On the 5th of March the prisoner came to lodge with me, and remained till the Wednesday following - as I was opening one of my boxes he saw some duplicates; in consequence of information I examined the duplicates again on the 19th of March - I missed eight of them; one was for a jacket, another for a pair of trousers, another for a shawl, another for some ear-rings, and some for other things.
WILLIAM HUDSON. I am an apprentice to Mr. Harrison, a watch-maker, in Upper East Smithfield. I purchased some duplicates of a person named Williams, who is gone to sea - I took the articles out of pawn.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310407-239]
934. JOHN BRADFORD was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of April, 2 crowns, 1 half-crown, and 1 sixpence, the monies of John Franks, alias John Francis, from his person.
JOHN FRANKS. I am a seaman, and lodge at the Dundee Arms, Wapping. On Saturday, the 3rd of April, I was at a public-house, and met a young woman, named Jane - I went with her to a house, where I saw the prisoner and two women; I then had 14s. 6d. in my pocket- the young woman was drunk; she invited me to go to the house - the prisoner asked me to play at cards with him, which I did; it was my deal - I took the cards in my hand, gave him six, and took six for myself; he let one of the cards fall on the table, and said it was a misdeal - we were playing at all-fours; the prisoner then said, "You d - d black b - r, you have got no money;" I put my hand into my left-hand waistcoat pocket, took out my money, and laid it on the table - he then gave me a sudden blow in the eye, and when I put my hand to my eye, he took up my money, put his hand into my pocket and tore it, but I had no money there; I went out of the house, and, as I did not know the number, I took out my knife, and cut four marks on the window-shutter- I got an officer, and the prisoner was taken sitting on a chair; I was sober.
THOMAS BATES. I am a Police-constable. At twenty minutes before two o'clock on the Sunday morning, the prisoner came to me - I went with him to No. 9, Matchwalk, Shadwell - I saw four cuts on the shutter - I went in,and saw the prisoner and three women - I was going to search the prisoner with Serjeant Garde, who was with me, when he pulled 15s. out of his pocket; he said it was his own - he put 14s. into the serjeant's hand, and 1s. fell on the table - he said he had changed a sovereign at the Green Dragon, Stepney, and then afterwards said at the Blue Anchor.
WILLIAM ROBERT GARDE. I am a Police-serjeant. I went to the house and saw the marks on the shutters - what has been said is correct.
Prisoner's Defence. I called to see a friend - the prosecutor was called in by one of the ladies; he drank out of a pot of porter, and they asked him to play at all fours; we played, he made a mis-deal, and I told him of it - he got up, put his hands across, and said, "I will not pay"- he pulled out no money, and I never saw a farthing of money while I was there - he went backwards, and was there half an hour with one of the women; we then played at cards, and when he got up and put his arm across, I just touched him a cross back-hander, and said, "I won't play any more, I don't think you have any money" - he is one of the biggest brutes that ever lived, to swear that I ever got off my seat - the woman he went home with says he had but 1s. 8 1/2d. in his pocket.
JANE HILLYER. I live at No. 9, Well's-row, Shadwell. On that Saturday night I went into the Ship and Shears -I saw the prosecutor there drinking a pint of beer; I went with him to the house where the prisoner was - I was not sober, and I was not very tipsy - I took the prosecutor into my room; he wanted to stay all night with me - I asked what he was going to give me; he looked into all his pockets and said all he had was 1s. 0 1/2d. - I said I could not stay with him for that - he then went out of my room, and did not give me any money.
EMMA BERRY. On that Saturday night the prosecutor came into my room, and said he was not going to stay with that young woman, as he had not money enough - he asked if I would stay with him, and he would pay me the next day - I said No - he then said he would have a game at cards for half-a-pint of gin; while we were playing, he began to cheat me, and then I would not play with him; he then said he would keep playing with the prisoner; he dropped a card, and said it was the prisoner who dropped it; some words arose between them; he called the prisoner some names - the prisoner got up and struck him, across the table, on the eye - there was nothing done to the prosecutor's pocket after he was struck, nor before, but what he did himself when he took out the 1s. 0 1/2d. and put them in again - when he was struck he got up, bade us good night, and went out - he came in again, stood before the prisoner, and said, "You have struck me on one side, now strike me on the other - the prisoner would not, and said he wanted to have nothing to say to him - he then went out, and returned with the officer.
ELIZABETH FOGWELL. The prisoner has lodged with me for nine months. On that Saturday he had been at work with my husband, and received a sovereign and a halfpenny, the same as my husband did, but I was not there; I saw him have the sovereign in his possession at six o'clock, and at seven he changed it at the Green Dragon, Stepney; he did not come home to my house that night - we were waiting for him; he is a coal-whipper.
ANN CHAMBERLAIN. The prisoner was at my house at seven o'clock that night with the silver in his hand; there appeared to be nearly 1l. worth, and there was a five shilling piece among it.
ANN IVES. I live at No. 2, James-street, Limehousefields. I am a tailoress; on that Saturday evening I saw the prisoner with some silver in his hand, which he took out to look for 1s. to send for some peppermint; I saw some half-crowns amongst it.
THOMAS BATES re-examined. Q. Before you took the prisoner, did the prosecutor state what money he had lost? A. Yes; he said he had lost 13s. 6d. or 14s. - I have inquired, and found the prisoner has got his living by coal-work till the last five months.
JURY. Q. Did the prosecutor state what his silver consisted of? A. Yes; he said a crown-piece, and some half-crowns - I found on the prisoner one crown, three half-crowns, two shillings, and one sixpence.
Prisoner. There was 1s. and 1 1/2d found on him, and that was all the money he had.
GUILTY of stealing only. Aged 38.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18310407-240]
935. WILLIAM BENJAMIN BRAY and CHARLES JOHN WALMSLEY were indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 9th of February, 9 sticks of timber, value 40l., the goods of John Chatfield and others, well knowing them to have been stolen; to which they both pleaded.
GUILTY. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18310407-241]
936. RICHARD THURBORNE was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of February, 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of Herbert Turner, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
HERBERT TURNER. I am a surgeon, and live in Dean-street, St. Thomas', Southwark. On the night of the 23rd of February, about half-past seven o'clock, I was in John-street, going home with a friend - I felt something at my coat pocket, but I did not turn immediately; I waited to have my pocket picked - I then turned, and saw the prisoner with my handkerchief in his hand, and another boy near him, who ran away - I caught the prisoner directly; I seized his hand and the handkerchief at the same moment- he said he was not the boy who picked the pocket, and wished me to let him go; he said the handkerchief was on the ground, and he picked it up - the officer came in a few minutes, and I gave the boy and the handkerchief to him.
JAMES BOYCE COX. I am the Police-constable. I received the prisoner on the 23rd of February; this is the handkerchief - it was partly in his hand, and partly in the prosecutor's.
Prisoner. The prosecutor had me in his possession a quarter of an hour before the officer came up; he had put the handkerchief into his pocket, and took it out again - he said he would do all he could to send me out of the country.
MR. TURNER. When I turned round the other boy ran away, and the other was about arm's length from me - I turned the instant I thought the handkerchief clear from my person - the prisoner was not running; he said he was innocent, and wished me to go to his mother's.
Prisoner's Defence (written). I beg to assert that I am innocent of the crime imputed to me; I was passing at the moment on an errand for my mother - I saw the handkerchief laying on the ground; I picked it up (thinking it had been dropped by some person), when I was seized and charged with stealing the handkerchief.
JOHN DOVE. I am a Police-constable. I know the prisoner; I saw him tried here on the 6th of January last- I am sure he is the same person; I have the certificate of his conviction - (read).
GUILTY. - Aged 15. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18310407-242]
937. GEORGE BLAKE was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of March, 1 chaise-rug, value 2s., the goods of John Cotton; and 1 pair of boots, value 2s. 6d., the goods of William Everett.
THOMAS JOSEPH WEST. I am a Police-constable. On the Tuesday before the 5th of March, I was in Hopkin-street, St. James; I saw the prisoner carry something under his arm, into an old iron-shop - I followed him, and saw he had this pair of boots and this chaise rug; he asked if they bought such things - the girl saw me behind him, and said they did not; I asked what he had got - he said an old rug which his master had given him to cut for horse-cloths - I went to inquire at a place he sent me to, and it was denied - I said to him the next morning, "It was useless for you to give me so much trouble last night;" he said, "I will not give you any more trouble; I took the rug from Howard's livery-stable, in Jermyn-street;" the boots, he said, were his own, and he was in distress - his wife and children appeared to be so.
WILLIAM EVERETT. I am coachman to Lord Romney, of York-street, St. James'. These boots are mine - I kept them in Howard's stable, in Jermyn-street; the prisoner had been at work for a few days there - I missed them the day after they were lost.
EDWARD ROBERTS. I am groom to John Cotton, Esq., of No.12, Carlton-chambers, Regent-street. This rug belongs to him; I did not miss it till the officer brought it me - I had seen the prisoner working two or three days at Howard's livery stables.
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 33. - Confined Two Days.
[trialAccount t18310407-243]
938. JOHN BOSTON and JAMES CLARK were indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 2s. 6d., the goods of a person unknown, from his person.
THOMAS JOSEPH WEST. I am a Police-constable. On the night of the 24th of February I was in company with Hobbs, in St. James-street, about nine o'clock - the two prisoners were pointed out to me by a person, named Dorrell - I saw a gentleman and lady there - Boston went behind the gentleman, and drew a handkerchief out of his left-hand pocket, took it in his left-hand, and put it behind him to the other prisoner - I put my arms round them, and called to Hobbs, "They have got it;" Hobbs came and took one - I took the other, and the handkerchief laid on the ground at their feet - it was an illumination night, and owing to the gangs that were about, it was impossible to get the gentleman without losing the prisoners.
THOMAS HOBBS. I am a Police-constable. I was with West - I saw the prisoners going up against gentlemen's pockets; we watched them some time - West called to me; I went up, put their feet on one side, and took up the handkerchief between the two prisoners.
GEORGE DORHELL. I saw the prisoners together on the night of the 24th of February - they attempted several pockets, and I mentioned it to the officers; I saw Boston take hold of a gentleman's pocket, put his hand into his pocket, and take out this handkerchief.
THOMAS HOBBS. This is the handkerchief. I asked Boston what was on it - he said he did not believe there was any thing on it, but there is J. C. on it.
Clark's Defence. There was a mob of two thousand people - the handkerchief was picked up behind two women; I was two yards from this young man - I had not been in the crowd two minutes before I was taken.
Boston's Defence. The officer caught hold of me first, I asked what he wanted, and he let me go - the J. C. are the initials of my second father's name.
JAMES COSGROVE. I am step-father to Boston. I have known him since he was three months old - he never knew his own name till lately, as he was always reared with me and served his time to me - he always went by my name till he was taken, and then he gave his right name.
JURY. Q. Had you any silk handkerchiefs? A. Yes, of different colours, but it is seldom I wear one - my son has taken them when he thought proper; I do not know whether I lost one or not.
BOSTON - GUILTY. Aged 18.
CLARK - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18310407-244]
939. WILLIAM CARR was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1 chaise-harness, value 40s., the goods of Samuel Ewins.
JAMES BOYCE COX. I am a Police-officer. On the night of the 4th of April I was in St. John-street - I saw the prisoner and another come out of the Pewter Platter-yard; the prisoner was carrying a set of harness on his shoulder; he crossed into Wilderness-row before I stopped him - I asked what he had there; he said it was his own, that he was going to take it home, and I might go with him and see where he took it to - I said it was of no consequence where he was going to take it to, but I wished to know where he got it; he then went down Pewter Platter-yard (there was another man with him); he threw down the harness under a cart, and said that was where he found it when he went down to a place of convenience - I took him to the station; but before I took him away, I saw a coach-house door open; when I had taken him away, I returned and got a light; I then found the coach-house door was broken open, and this piece of a lock was inside.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. He took you to the yard where he said he found the harness? A. Yes, but I had seen him come out there - this was about half-past twelve o'clock.
JESSE EAST. I am in the service of Mr. Samuel Ewins, a bookbinder, who lives in Duke-street, Smithfield - he has a coach-house in Pewter Platter-yard. On the afternoon of the 4th of April I fastened the door of the coach-house, leaving this harness inside it - I found the door broken open, and the padlock broken; this is his harness.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know where the barness was? A.When I went home I left it in the coach-house, at half-past five o'clock.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18310407-245]
940. ANN CREATON was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of March, 8 stockings, value 4s.; 2 gloves, value 1s; 3 wristbands, value 6d.; 1 petticoat, value 2s. and 3 pieces of ribbon, value 3d., the goods of George Riddle.
CHRISTIANA RIDDLE. I am the wife of George Riddle - he keeps the White Swan, at Shadwell ; the prisoner lived servant with me for eleven months, and left on the 16th of February. I missed a few articles while she was with me, but I was ill in the country in the summer, and when I came home I did not ask any one in the house about them - I missed some silk handkerchiefs and a coral necklace from a box in my bed-room; I had the necklace safe on New Year's Day, and on the Friday before, the 11th of March, I missed it - I went to ask the prisoner about it; she pleaded her innocence.
JAMES MORGAN. I am a Police-serjeant. In consequence of information I went to the prisoner; she delivered her keys to me, and told me she lodged at No. 1, Twine-court; I went with the prosecutor there; I opened a box with a key the prisoner gave me; some articles there were claimed by the prosecutor - I brought them away; I told the prisoner what I had found; she said at first there was nothing belonging to her master, and then she said they were what her mistress gave her - here are four pairs of stockings, a petticoat, one pair of gloves, some wristbands, and some ribbon.
MRS. RIDDLE. These came from the wash while I was in the country, and I believe she took them only till they were owned - they are my property, and I never gave them to her; there is no mark on them - this petticoat I can swear to: it was sent to wash while I was in the country - I had returned four months when she was apprehended - here is a pair of gloves, but no mark on them; I have never found my necklace.
Prisoner's Defence. My mistress may forget, but she gave me that old petticoat before she went into the country.
MRS. RIDDLE. I do not remember it, but I could not swear I did not - she was not in the country with me.
NOT GUILTY.

941. WILLIAM DAVISON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 25 yards of cotton, value 25s. the goods of Edward Hill; and that he had before been convicted of felony.
HENRY LENNON. I am a Police-constable. At half-past nine o'clock on the night of the 24th of February I saw the prisoner near Shadwell-market, by a door in an alley; he had his jacket and waistcoat off, and his shirt-sleeves turned up - I asked what he was doing; he said he was a sailor, and was going to sleep down there - I found his jacket and waistcoat were over this piece of cotton; when he took the waistcoat up to put it on I saw the cotton - I found the prosecutor's shop about three hundred yards from the place.
WILLIAM GOULBOURN. I am in the employ of Mr. Edward Hill - he is a linen-draper, of High-street, Shadwell. This cotton is his property - it has two marks on it, in my own hand-writing; I had seen it safe about six o'clock in the evening, and it was brought to the shop between nine and ten o'clock.
CHARLES WALTER WHEELER. I know the prisoner; I saw him here last Session - he was tried, and pleaded starvation; I know he is the man - I got this certificate of his conviction from Mr. Clark's office - (read).
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.

942. MARY HOWE was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of March, 1 coat, value 8s; 2 blankets, value 1s. 6d; 1 sheet, value 3s.; 1 shirt, value 3s.; 1 frock, value 1s., and 1 apron, value 6d., the goods of Michael Hanley.
CATHERINE HANLEY. I am the wife of Michael Hanley - we lodge on the first floor in George-street, Islington. On the afternoon of the 15th of March, I returned home, and missed the prisoner, who had been employed to take care of my place and my children, in my absence - I also missed some articles.

943. THOMAS LEADBETTER was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of February, 1 pair of breeches, value 7s., and 1 pair of trousers, value 6s, the goods of Samuel Lowe.
MARY ANN LOWE. I am the wife of Samuel Lowe - he lives in Brown's-lane, Spitalfields, and is a tailor. On the Saturday before the 28th of February I was in the shop, and saw the prisoner about for a quarter of an hour; I went into my back parlour, to my little girl, who had fallen down, and on coming into the shop again, I missed these articles - the trousers had been on the doorpost, and the breeches near the door; I had put them there not long before - I went out, and missed the prisoner; I went in again, and sat down some time, when I thought I saw him on the other side of the way, with a bundle under his arm- I looked, and saw the strings and buttons of the smallclothes hanging out of the bundle; I called the Policeman, who ran, and took him - these are the articles; the breeches are worth 7s. 2d. and the trousers 6s. 3d.
WILLIAM BEALE. I am a Police-serjeant. I was in Brown's-lane, and in consequence of what this lady said, I pursued, and took the prisoner - he was running, with this bundle under his arm.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was in Spitalfields-market, and a young man told me to go with him for a parcel; hewent and brought this bundle - he told me to hold it while he tied his shoe; I heard a cry of Stop thief! and ran.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Confined Three Months.

944. THOMAS MASON was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 4 shawls, value 4l., the goods of Charles Earith, his master.
CHARLES EARITH. I am a silk-dyer, and live in Goswell-street. The prisoner was in my employ a fortnight; I missed some articles, and in consequence of information, I gave him into custody - I have since seen four shawls, worth a guinea each, at a pawnbroker's - I missed one on the 17th of March, and one on the 19th.
CHARLES WORLEY. I am foreman to Mr. Cassel, a pawnbroker, in Old-street. I produce a crape shawl, pawned at our house by the prisoner, in the name of Thomas Mason, for 4s. - he said he came from his father: he came again with this other shawl 1-1 questioned him again- I was not satisfied with his answers - I told him to go and get his father; he went away.
HENRY JEROME. I am shopman to Mr. Jones, a pawnbroker, in Wood-street. Spitalfields. I have two crope shawls, pawned by the prisoner on the 17th of March - I advanced him 5s. on each of them, and then 2s. more on one of them - he had been there before, for his parents.
GUILTY. Aged 14. - Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18310407-250]
945. RICHARD MILLER was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 1 trunk, value 4s.; 1 cloak, value 1s., 3 jackets, value 5s.; 1 coat, value 10s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 3s.; 3 shirts, value 6s; 2 fronts, value 1s. 6d.; 5 waistcoats, value 8s.; 2 pairs of drawers, value 3s.; 4 handkerchiefs, value 10s; 2 pairs of stockings, value 1s., and 2 caps, value 2s. 6d. the goods of Henry Turner.
2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Benjamin Walker.
3rd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Richard Wells.
4th COUNT, stating them to be the goods of a certain person or persons unknown.
BENJAMIN WALKER. I keep the Bell and Crown public-house, in Kingsland-road . On the 24th of March William Wells, a carrier, brought this trunk to my house, directed for Mr. Henry Turner, to be left at the Bell and Crown, public-house, Hoxton, until called for - on the Saturday morning the prisoner came and brought this note, (read) -
Mr. Turner will thank the landlord of the Bell and Crown to let the bearer have the box left by the Hoxton carrier - I consider him very neglectful in not leaving it at my house yesterday, as the young man told me it would be delivered by one o'clock yesterday.
Bath-street, March 24, 1831.
Directed to Mr. Walker, Bell and Crown, Kingsland-road. I said I thought it very strange, as two persons had been for it before, I would go and get the carrier - I went and got an officer, and when I came back the prisoner was gone; I saw him again in company with the officer - I let him have the trunk, and charged him 5s. 3d. for it; he said he was going to take it to the corner of the mad-house, but he wished us to go with him - I went on with him and the officer: as we were going on I said to the prisoner,"Suppose you don't meet the person you expect at the corner of the mad-house, where are you going to take the trunk?" he said, "I shall take it to No. 22, Bath-street, and ask for 1s.:" there was no man at the corner, and we went to No. 22, Bath-street - they said they knew nothing of the trunk nor the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. He never varied in his account of the transaction? A. No - he said the man had given him 5s. 6d. to pay for the trunk; it was a half-crown and three shillings, and I gave him 3d. change; the trunk came on the Thursday; I had two persons apply for it on Friday - the prisoner came on Saturday; it should have been left at Hoxton before it arrived at my house - the prisoner did not state to me, on leaving my house, that he was going to get a bit of steak to have it dressed at the house; he did not return till he was brought back by the officer - I had told the officer a man had come for the box, and I told him to go and take care of it; I understood when the officer went there the prisoner went out, and he went after him - he found him about one hundred yards from the house; I saw him find him - he had no meat with him; I suppose I was about five minutes gone for the officer - I did not leave any one to take care of the prisoner; I said I was going for the carrier - the box was in the house at the time, but I believe the prisoner did not see it; it was before the box had been delivered to him, that I went for the officer - I then delivered him the box, for the purpose of seeing where he would take it; he then said he would not take it unless some one went with him.
HENRY TURNER. I know this trunk - it contains articles belonging to a brother of mine, who is dead; I saw the trunk about two years ago - I expected the trunk and articles from Edinburgh.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18310407-251]
946. JAMES REYNOLDS was indicted for bigamy.
JAMES GOOD. I am sexton, and assistant parish-clerk, at St. Bride's, in the city of London. I produce the register-book of the marriages in that parish - here is the register of the marriage of James Reynolds and Ann Emery, on the 7th of March, 1824; it is signed by the clergyman of the parish.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. I believe you were not in office at that time? A. No.
CHARLOTTE BUCKNEY. I am the wife of Thomas Buckney - he is a grocer, and lives in York-street, Batter, sea-fields. I was present at the marriage of the prisoner and my sister Ann Emery; my name was then Charlotte Emery - the prisoner is the person who married her; she is alive, and is in Court now.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you known the prisoner before his marriage? A. yes, a few months.
SARAH CADBY. I lived servant at No. 18, Upper Grosvenor-street. I was married to the prisoner on the 28th of December last, at St. George, Hanover-square.
Cross-examined. Q.How long had you known him? A. Six months or more - he told me he was a compositor, but he was a newspaper vender; I had no fortune - I did not know he had ever been married; I do not know Joseph Bates - I never told him, or said in his presence, that I knew the prisoner had been married; but after I was married a person said to me, "James Reynolds is married;""Yes, (said I) I know he is, he is married to me;" I nowlive with my father - I do not know that he threatened the prisoner with any violence; the prisoner put the marriage off once for three weeks - he said he wanted to go into the country.
COURT. Q. How long after you were married did you find out that he had been married? A. I suppose about two months - my father found it out, but I had gone into service before that, and the prisoner said he was going to live with his father.
JOSEPH CADBY. I am the father of Sarah Cadby. I have a certificate of her marriage at St. George's, Hanover-square; I examined it with the book at the church - I took the prisoner on the 24th of February - (certificate read.)
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you have been some time a constable? A. Yes, but I had not the least idea of this till the 24th of February - my daughter went to service after the marriage, unknown to me; I never said I suspected the prisoner was married before this marriage - I only saw him twice before this marriage; he told me he was an apprentice, and did not wish his friends to know it; I asked him to step into a house, that I might look at him, and I said, "I think you are rather an old apprentice, and if I thought you were about to take any advantage of a child of mine, I should no more mind blowing out your brains than looking at you:" this was only a few nights before the marriage - I asked him for a reference, and he sent me to a place in Wych-street; they would not tell me much of him; and when I saw him again, he said the reason was that he was a natural child; I was going to the church to forbid the banns, but I was too late - I met them coming back.
SARAH CAMINI. I accompanied Sarah Cadby when she was married to the prisoner, on the 28th of December, 1830.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 28. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18310407-252]
947. RICHARD WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
GEORGE DORRELL. I am a brush-maker, and live in Carnaby-street. On the Thursday before the 17th of March, I was in Cleveland-row; there was a great crowd- I saw the prisoner there - he attempted two or three persons' pockets before he succeeded in taking a handkerchief; I at last saw him take one from a gentleman's pocket, and put it into his own left-hand trousers pocket; I told Hobbs, who drew his hand out, with the handkerchief in it, before he had time to put it away.
Prisoner. Q. How far were you from me? A. Close to you; I told the officer as soon as I saw you take the handkerchief; You made no resistance - I unbuttoned your pocket, and threw out two handkerchiefs; the crowd was so great, it was impossible to get to the gentleman - I mentioned it to him, then on turning to secure you, I lost sight of him; the gentleman was not so tall as you, to the best of my recollection, not very stout, nor very thin; I believe he had a black body coat on - you were taken to the station-house, but no handkerchiefs were found on you there- I believe three or four were found on you in going along, besides the one you are indicted for; you told the Magistrate you should reserve what you had to say till another time - you was then committed for a week, on the evidence of Hobbs, myself, and another person, who picked up the handkerchief, but who was not bound over; the deposition was taken on the 17th of March - it made no difference to me whether you were committed on suspicion or not; I get my subsistence by working at my trade; I do not attend Police-offices - I did not know the officers; I did not say any such word as "Here is one, John;" when the Policemen got round you, I went back to find the gentleman, but he was gone; I do not know what I shall receive here - I did not say on the first examination that I apprehended you on suspicion; my name was put foremost at Clerkenwell Session, or I should not have prosecuted - I was examined as a witness.
THOMAS HOBBS. I am a Police-constable. On the Thursday before the 17th of March I was on duty at the bottom of St. James'-street; I saw the prisoner, and watched him some time; he went up against gentlemen's pockets, and when he got thicker into the crowd, I saw him with the handkerchief in his hand, just behind a gentleman; I seized him, and pulled his hand out of his pocket, with the handkerchief in it - there were three other handkerchiefs found on him in going to the station-house, and a snuff-box; I said to him, "How came an old man like you to do this?" he said, "What is a poor fellow to do?"
Prisoner. Q. What reply did I make when you said to the other man, "I thought if I did not know him you would?" A. I never heard it said by myself or any one else; when I took hold of you I threw you into a clear place, and the only word I said to you was, "An old man like you, how came you to do it," and your reply was, "What is a poor fellow to do?" your right-hand pocket was ripped open, so that what was put into it might go down; the man you took it from might be rather stouter than you, and between thirty and forty he had a close body coat on; I cannot say what colour; I do not suppose he was more than two yards off you; no sooner had I seized you, than the swell mob came round to try to get you away, and we were surrounded by the Policemen: there were four handkerchiefs produced before the Magistrate - you were very likely committed to Tothill-fields, I do not know; the charge was for picking pockets.
Prisoner's Defence. I have not the means of procuring the document from the Police-office, and it appears now that your Lordship has only the second depositions; the depositions taken on the 10th of March were to the effect, solely that these men arrested me on suspicion, and found the handkerchiefs and snuff-box on my person - all the documents ought to be before your Lordship; and if a legal process takes place, and a man is put into prison, and brought before a superior tribunal, surely you will not hesitate to send for this first document; there it is in black and white, "Receive into your custody so and so, on suspicion of feloniously taking," &c.; cases of this nature are so common and notorious, that they have been scouted from Westminster-hall, and therefore it is that they have come here - these men have scouted the charge of suspicion, and now bring the charge of felony; and why or wherefore? because they found I was poor and friendless, or I should have discovered that the other was nothing but a common hireling, ready to come on any occasion; the difference to them is the difference of mygoing to prison, and the money they will get for coming here a second time and charging a felony; but will your Lordship upheld the doctrine, and countenance the practice that a man sworn to execute the law should one day apprehend a prisoner, and swear to one fact, and then a week afterwards swear to a contrary fact? Can two contrary facts both be true? one of them must necessarily be false-will your Lordship condescend to send for the depositions taken on the 10th of March, and also for the original warrant of commitment to Tothill-fields.
THOMAS HOBBS re-examined. Q. Was the charge on suspicion of felony? A. Yes; I charged him with picking the pocket of a gentleman unknown - the Magistrate has sent all the depositions.
Prisoner. Surely your Lordship must wish to administer justice, and surely the delay of half an hour cannot be of importance. There is the document in the office, and here are twelve gentlemen come to swear that they will return a true verdict. If the Magistrate had done his duty, you would have had the depositions taken on 10th of March.
COURT. We have now got your commitment, and it is precisely in the form of the present charge, for stealing a handkerchief from a man unknown.
Prisoner. That is the reason why I asked if the whole four handkerchiefs were produced before the Magistrate, and I was charged on suspicion of them all - only a mere bailable charge; and you can have that evidence before you in half an hour; and surely, however depressed I may be, I have a right to justice. I hope your Lordship will send an officer for it, who is not interested in suppressing it.
JURY to THOMAS HOBBS. Q. How long have you known Dorrell? A. Very likely twelve months - I have seen him, but not to speak to him, before that day.
Prisoner to GEORGE DORRELL. Q. How long was it after you saw this taken that you seized me? A. Directly, and I prevented it from going to the bottom of your pocket-I do not know that I unbuttoned your pocket;I might pull your hand away from it - I did not feel your pocket.
Prisoner. If this doctrine is held forth that a witness may come one day and swear one fact, and another day a directly contrary fact, what individual could he safe? The witness charges me one day on suspicion of felony -I suspect he stole it" - and a week after he comes and days,"I charge him with stealing it; I saw him steal it." In the first instance more than half the truth was kept back, or in the second more than the truth was stated. I was passing the Green-park, saw a person look around him, and place something at the root of a tree; I pretended not to observe him; but when he went away, I went and found the handkerchiefs and a snuff-box - I went on, and was obstructed by the crowd; a hand pressed on my thigh, and a voice exclaimes, "John, here is one of them," the same person unbuttoned my pocket, and took out tow handkerchiefs, which I had put into that pocket, and I had two in the other pocket - I was then taken to the office, and there these men stated they arrested me on suspicion - the Magisrate asked if they had seen me take any thing; they said No. I do not know whether it was the same magistrate that I saw on the 17th, but if so, he had forgotten it, and I did not think it my business to put him in mind of it - they had in the interim found that I was poor and friendless, and they said, "Let us change the ground, and make a direct theft of it, and have a day or two's lounge-City fare is pretty good," and here they are, from the first day to the last or the last but one, and can any man be astonished that a man should come here to swear for 30s.or 40s, when it is well known that there are hundreds and thousands who will swear for half a crown.
GUILTY. Aged 56. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18310407-253]
Fifth Middlesex Jury-before Mr. Common Sergeant
948. JOHN HARRINGTON was indicted for feloniously forging a false request for delivery of goods, with intent to defraud James Shoolbred and Gregory Cook.
SECOND COUNT, for feloniously uttering the same.
SAMUEL CUTTER. I live with Messrs. James Shoolbred and Gregory Cook - they are haberdashers and linendrapers, and live in Tottenham-court-road; they have a customer named Brewer-I know the prisoner, and have received three orders from him; I believe he was in the employ of Mr. Wyat, in whose family Miss Brewer lived as governess. On the 7th of March the prisoner brought this paper to my master's:-
MR. CUTTER. - If you have got seven yards in one piece of the same kind you sent on Saturday, but not to be entered to Mr. Wyat's account, I will pay for it on Wednesday, and about three skeins of silk to match it, tow sheets of wadding, and three yards of black ribbon half an inch in width; and if you can send the bill to-day, as we want to pay our quarter's bills.
March 7,1831. Miss BREWER.
In consequence of this I gave the prisoner the goods as near the order as possible - I had not seven yards of silk, so I sent a remnant of ten yards and a half; I had sent two yards and a half of black silk, and three-quarters of a yard of crimson on the Saturday - the ten and a half I sent was black, as that is a more general article; I also sent four skeins of sewing silk, and I think two sheets of wadding and three yards of black ribbon, half an inch wide, as ordered; I did that, believing the signature to be genuine, and the hand-writing of the lady who was governess in Mr. Wyat's family - I have many times before acted upon a written order from that lady; Mr. Wyat soner is the person who had the things.
JANE BREWER. I am governess at Mr Wyat's, at Dudley-grove, Paddington. No part of this note is my writing, nor was it written by my authority - the prisoner was servant to Mr. Wyat, and I think was discharged about a month or six weeks ago; He had been there nine or ten months - Mr.Wyat is a sculptor, and the prisoner was his errand-boy; I believe he bore an honest character - he was discharged before the day in question.
CHARLES MAINE. This black silk was pawned with me on the 7th of March for 1l. 2s. and, to the best of my belief, by the prisoner; this is the counterpart of the duplicate - there are ten yards and a half of it.
GEORGE McGREGER. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoner on the 7th of March - I found this sewing silk an ribbon in his pocket; he had come back to the prosecutor's shop for more goods.
SAMUEL CUTTER. I know this silk from the length.and from the prisoner stating where he had pawned it -I went there, and found it; it corresponds in quality - I believe this sewing silk and ribbon to be what I gave according to the order.
JURY. Q. What time was the order produced? A. About half - past nine or ten o'clock on Monday morning; Miss Brewer did not keep a separate account - it was entered to Mr, Wyat, as he discharged any thing that Miss Brewer had.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18310407-254]
949. MICHAEL WALDON was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 1 plane, value 3s., the goods of Thomas Martin.
THOMAS MARTIN. I live in Lower-street, Islington, and am a carpenter. On the 12th of March I lost a plane from my bench, in an unfinished house in Chadwell-street - I left it safe at dinner-time, and did not miss it till about four o'clock; the prisoner was employed on the same premises - this is the plane.
HENRY FOWLER. I the service of Mr. Cordwell, a pawnbroker, in Exmouth-street, on the 12th of March the prisoner brought this plane to out shop - he pawned it for 6d, in the name of John Davies, for his father; he came twice to the shop with it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you not on the first occasion doubt whether this was the person?
A. I did not recognize him in the first instance, but that was on a different charge; his brother was brought up, and while he was gone for his brother another person asked if I knew him, and I said No - there is a brotherly likeness between them; I believe he is older, but not so tall- I did not express a doubt whether the prisoner was the person who pawned the plane; I did not entertain any doubt about that.
THOMAS LOW. I am a constable. I took the prisoner into custody.
Prisoner's Defence. The pawnbroker said when I went with my brother, that I was the lad who pawned it. and I was not - he said I went there between three and five O'clock.
THOMAS MARTIN re-examined. Q. Did you know the prisoner's brother? A. Yes - he never worked there but one day, which was after the 12th of March, when his brother was ill - I think the pawnbroker's shop is half a mile from where I worked.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 1 Month.

950. GEORGE THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 1 tea-kettle, value 1s,6d. the goods of Richard Jones.
WRIGHT WOODHOUSE. I live with Mr. Richard Jones - he keeps a public-house at the corner of Plumtree-street and Broad-street, St. Giles'. I used the tea-kettle on the 28th of March, about seven o'clock in the morning; the prisoner came in about that time - I missed the kettle between half-past seven and eight o'clock; I had put it down by the side of the pump, in the corner, over the counter - I went on with my work, and when I came to look for the kettle, I missed it.
WILLIAM HARRIS. I am a Police-constable. On the 28th of March a latl came running to me from a shop to take a man with a kettle; I went to take him for stealing other things-he had this kettle, which he said was given to him by woman to sell for 1s.
JURY. Q. Had he the kettle in his hand? A. No - it was on the counter, where he offered it for sale; it was stated in his presence that he had offered it.(Properly produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defenee. A woman met me at the corner of George-street, and asked me to sell this kettle for her - I went to try to sell it, and the woman sent for the officer.
GUILTY. Aged 21. - Confined Six Months.

CLARA COLLYER. I am single. I keep a boot and shoe-shop in St. Martin's-court. The officer brought the prisoner and the shoes to my shop about five o'clock - they are mine, and are worth 6s; I had seen them safe about half an hour before.

952. GEORGE STONE was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of February, 2 loaves of bread, value 1s. 4d. the goods of John Elwin.
ARN ELWIN. I am the wife of John Elwin - he lives in Wellington-street, Bethnal-green, and is a baker, On the 25th of February I was in a little room which joins the shop - there is a glass window between them; I saw the prisoner, who had just taken two loaves from the window, and was walking out with them - I opened the door, and called to him; he put them on the threshold, and ran away - I am quite sure he is the person; Mr Gardiner brought him back - put the same loaves on the counter, and the policeman took them.
EDMUND GARDINER. I live in Wellington-street, Bethnal-green. On the 25th of February I was in Selby-street - the prosecutor's shop is at the corner; I saw the prisoner running fast from their door - the prosecutrix came to the shop, and called to me to stop him, which I did.
MICHAEL JOURDAN. I am a Police-constable. I was sent for, and took the prisoner - I received two loaves from the prosecutrix and her husband.

953. CHARLES ROBERTS was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 1 jacket, value 5s., and 1 pair of trousers, value 6s., the goods of William Hall, from the person of Henry Hall .
DAVID DILLON. I am six years and a half old - I go to church sometimes; I live with my father, in Norwich-court, Fetter-lane. I remember playing with Henry Hall at the corner of Graystoke-place - the prisoner came, and told Henry Hall to go and find his donkey; he went, and the prisoner went after him - when they got to the top of Fetter-lane, I saw the prisoner take Henry Hall on his shoulder, and he went away; Hall had his clothes on then, his pinafore, shoes and stockings - I saw him in the evening when he came home; he had only his shirt, shoes and pinafore, and his sister's pelisse on - the prisoner had a black coat on at first; when I saw him again he had a white coat.
WILLIAM HALL. I live at No. 2, Norwich-court, and am an ornamental painter - my son Henry in six years old. I saw him on the 14th of February; he had on his stockings, his boots, a shirt, a suit of cloth clothes, a pinafore over them, and a cap; I lost sight of him about two o'clock when I went to my business, and did not return till five -I saw my son again about six o'clock: he then had on his stockings, his boots, a pinafore, and his sister's pelisse, which was sent to fetch him home in; his suit of clothes were gone.
Prisoner. Q. Did you ever see me? A. Not to my knowledge.
GEORGE HALL. I saw my brother at dinner time, with a dark suit of clothes on - he was playing in the court at one o'clock; I afterwards went to the White Horse, in Twister's-alley, Whitecross-street - I saw him there in the kitchen, without jacket or trousers on; I brought him home.
SUSAN SEVERIDGE. I am the wife of Anthony Severidge, of Graham's-building, Blue Anchor-alley, Bunhill-row. I found Henry Hall on the Monday, in the privy of the yard belonging to the apartment - he had only his shirt, shoes, and stockings on, and his cap and pinafore in his hand; I took him in doors, and he told me where his parents lived - this was about four o'clock, and I suppose a mile and a half from his parents.
NOT GUILTY. (See page 416.)

956. JAMES TURNEY was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 1 watch, value 2l., and 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of Henry Doubleday.
HENRY DOUBLEADAY. I am a private in the Royal Artillery; the prisoner belonged to the same corps, and lodged in the same room at Woolwich. On the 13th of March I went out, and left a silver watch, wrapped up in a white shirt, in my box- I returned about eight o'clock, and missed my watch and 5s.6d. in money; the prisoner was than missing - I saw him in about four days afterwards, at Woolwich; I lost two double handkerchiefs - I had left the money in my knapsack; no one was present when I put it away.
THOMAS RIDPATH. I am a pawnbroker, in Upper Seymour-street, Euston-square. The watch was pawned with me on the 19th of March, by Thomas Hintridge for 17s.
THOMAS HINTRIDGE. I live at the Southampton Arms, Camden-town. The prisoner came into the room, and had a pot of porter - he offered a watch for sale, and asked 25s. for it; I offered him 1s - he said he would not take 1l., he could pawn it for that; he went out, and the next day he returned, produced a duplicate, and offered it me for half a crown - I gave him 1s. 6d.for it; I sent Richard Griffin for the watch.
RICHARD GRIFFIN. I received the duplicate - I went to Battle-bridge, and took the watch out of pawn, for a guinea and 6d; I gave it to Hintridge.
THOMAS HINTRIDGE. I kept it two days, and then pawned it for 17s. at Mr. Ridpath's.
HENRY DOUBLEDAY. I can swear to this handkerchief by the pattern and colour - I know the watch by two cracks on the face; I have worn it some years.
Q. When did you see it again? A. on Wednesday last, and I lost it a month ago last Sunday - it has two seals and a key, which I know.
GUILTY. Aged 23 - Transported for Seven years.

957. CAROLINE STEVENS was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, 1 pocket-book, value 2d.; 1 needle-case, value 1d.; 1 cap, value 1s. 6d.; 1 lock and key,value 9d; 2 shillings, 2sixpences, and 12 penny-pieces, the property of John Lynch, from the person of Sarah, his wife.
SARAH LYNCH. I am the wife of John Lynch - we live in Riding-house-lane, Great Titchfield-street. On the 29th of March, I was at the Two Brewers, Great St. Andrew-street, Seven-dials - my husband and I had a little difference, and he knocked my cap off; I left him, and went to a doctor to have my head dressed - while I was at the public-house, the prisoner came up to the door; she asked what was the matter - I told her to put my bonnet and cap on my head; she took my lace cap, and put a dirty night-cap on my head - I had not been drinking freely: we had had a pint of beer - I had handed my pocket to my little girl - It had in it four duplicates, the key of my door, a pocket - book, four shillings, two sixpences, some copper, and a needle-case; she is not five years old - I gave it her to hold while I rolled up my hair; I saw the Prisoner snatch it from the child and run away.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not treat me to some gin? A. No - I gave her 3d. after she put the bonnet on my head; I did not then know she had changed my cap - my husband and I were not throwing the pocket at each other.
WILLIAM JACKSON. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoner, and some other persons on the 29th of March- she was at a public-house, at the corner of Monmouth-street; she dropped a needle-case and a pocket-book, which I took up after wards - I then saw her drop the key of the prosecutor's door; I did not find the cap.
Prisoner. I had nothing to do with the key - she was throwing the pocket at her husband, and Mary Riley put her bonnet and cap on; I asked if I should take her little girl to her husband, as she was so much intoxicated.
WILLIAM JACKSON. She was not intoxicated.
SARAH LYNCH. This is the night-cap, but I had had on a lace cap, drawn with pink ribbons, and two rows of edging.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY of stealing the needle-case, pocket-book, and key. Aged 26. - Confined one month.
[trialAccount t18310407-263]
958. WILLIAM STRIDE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 10 window-sashes, value 50s., the goods of Andrew Germain and another.
HENRY SWAIN. I am a Police-constable. On the morning of the 5th of April I saw some sashes behind a caravan, in the road near the Small Pox-hospital - Souter was standing by them; I afterwards saw the prisoner and Minifi, who were in conversation, but I could not tell what they said; I went up, and took the prisoner and Souter - the prisoner said he had bought the sashes on the 4th of April, at a sale over the water, but he could not tell the place, or the auctioneer's name - he gave me his address, which I found correct; the people said he had lived there eight months, but they did not know how he got his living.
Cross-examined by MR. STURGEON. Q. It is not an uncommon thing to have sales over the water? A. No; I did not see the sashes in any cart.
CHARLES SOUTER. I am porter, and lodge in King-street, Borough. On the 4th of April I was standing to be hired at London-bridge; the prisoner came and engaged me to carry some sash-frames to Battle-bridge the next morning, and the next morning, at half-past five, or a quarter before six o'clock, he took me to Joiner-street, Tooley-street - there were gates and a yard there; the bar was down - the prisoner opened the gates; I saw these sashframes there, on a tank, and the prisoner helped them on my knot - I carried them; I asked him whether I should go over London-bridge, as that was the nearest way, but he said he wanted to go to the Borough-market - I went on, he with me, and when we got to a joiner's shop, I pitched them down, and untied them - he went to a man named Minifi; the Policeman came, and took me and the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q.Then you were taken as well as the prisoner? A. Yes; I said he took them out of the yard, and so he did - I was not afraid I should be tried; I am a jobbing porter - I hand known the prisoner before; he had not paid me - I often do jobs without knowing what I shall have for them: the sashes were all tied up when he opened the gates, and he took them.
THOMAS MINIFI. I live at No.14, Battle-bridge, and am a glazier. The prisoner came to my house on the morning of the 5th of April, about eight o'clock, and asked if I wanted to buy some old windows; I said I did not know that I did, but where were they - he said, "Over here;" I went to look at them, and had some conversation with him - I said they did not suit me; the Policeman came up just as I left them.
PETER PAYTON. I am in the service of Mr. Andrew Germain and Mr. Fletcher - they are iron-merchants, and live in Iron-yard, Tooley-street. I have seen these ten sashes - they are my masters; I cannot tell what they are worth - they had been kept in the first floor warehouse near the counting - house; there was a considerable quantity of goods there, and these were behind the other things I went about ten o'clock in the morning of the 5th of April, and missed them; there was a lock on the back gate adjoining Joiner-street, and that had been forced, and then they must have crossed the premises; I should not have known how the gate could have been opened after the lock was off, till the Policeman showed me how the chain could be slipped over the bar; I had no suspicion of the sashes being lost till the policeman came - they would have been in the loft for twelve months; there was nothing removed on the Monday evening.
Cross-examined by Mr. PHILLIPS. Q. Were they new sashes? A. No, old ones, purchased at the old buildings in a street adjoining Tolley-street, some months ago - I do not know when; I had seen them in the loft very likely a month before - there were three blank panes in one of them- I have seen the prisoner at the premises - he came and said he was doing business for this gentleman, and that gentleman; he came there under pretence of buying, and laid out perhaps a few pence - I took his money, but it was very little.
PHILIP ROGERS. I am a carpenter, and live in Watney-street. I have looked at these sashes, and know them - I was employed to by them, and take them out; they were put into a loft on the prosecutors' premises - there is an entrance from joiner-street, and gates to the yard; there is an iron tank there.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You did not make these sashes? A. No, they are old ones - I know them by this moulding, and this little leaden light, which opens; you will not find another sash mended like this in London - I had observed it in those I bought.
GUILTY. Aged 38. - Confined Six Months.

961. SARAH WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 1 bonnet, value 6d.; 1 shawl, value 6d.; 1 pair of shoes, value 2s.; 1 pair of pattens, value 6d.; and 1 handkerchief, value 1s. the goods of Michael Mack.
CATHERINE MACK. I am the wife of Michael Mack, we live in Mulberry-row, Carnaby-market. On the 8th of April, about four o'clock in the afternoon, I was at the White Bear public-house - I had been taken ill at my work, went in there and sat down; the prisoner came in and sat by me - I had never seen her before - I had half a pint of beer in my hand - she was kind to me, and said she would see me home; I declined that; she offered again, and went home with me to my lodgings - she said I had better lay down as I was poorly; I did so, and fell asleep, leaving her to mind the room - I awoke in about an hour and she was gone - I missed my bonnet and these other things off a line - I found her about eight o'clock the next night, sitting in the same public-house, with my bonnet on; she said she had bought it of me, and had left her own in the room - there was no bonnet left there - I have found nothing else.
WILLIAM GIBBS. I am a porter. The prosecutrix fetched me to the public-house - I took the prisoner to the watch-house with this bonnet on her head.
Prisoner's Defence. There were ten or a dozen people saw me buy the bonnet of her at the public-house - I put it on, and she wore mine - the Magistrate sent her to see if it was not at her lodging, but she had made away with it - when I took her home her husband was there; he said he wished I had not brought her, for she was a drunken beast and made away with every thing.
CATHERINE MACK. I was not drunk, but quite ill - my husband came home and went out, leaving her there.
WILLIAM GIBBS. Directly I took the prisoner, she said she had bought the bonnet of her, and had seen nothing of the other things.
NOT GUILTY.

964. DANIEL DONOGHUE was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of April, 1 silver spoon, value 4s., the goods of Samuel Burchfield.
SAMUEL BURCHFIELD. I am a rope-maker, and live at Poplar; the prisoner was in my service. On the 3rd of April - the Policeman came to my house with a spoon, which I believe to be mine - I have brought one here, which corresponds with it - I understood from my servantthat one was missing, but she is not here - the prisoner was in the habit of coming into the house at times - he had been two or three years in my employ.
WILLIAM REEVES. I am a Policeman. On the 4th of April I was fetched to Mr. Young's - he gave the prisoner in charge, with this spoon - I asked how he came by it - he said he had been playing at buttons and marbles with some boys in Limehouse-causeway, and gave the buttons and marbles he had won of them, with 2d., for the spoon - he said he did not know the boys.
ROBERT YOUNG. I am a pawnbroker. On the 4th of April the prisoner came to my shop, and offered this spoon to pawn; I asked who he brought it from; he said from his mother - he gave several accounts about it, and at last I said, "I suppose you brought it from some boy?" - he said he did, and I gave him in charge.
SAMUEL BURCHFIELD. My initials are on the spoon -I have one here marked the same way, and I believe it to be mine - I always found the prisoner honest.
Prisoner's Defence. I played with some boys, and won some marbles of them - they asked me to buy the spoon; I gave the marbles and 2d. for it.
GUILTY. Aged 13. - Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18310407-270]
965. JOHN HADISTON and PETER GARRATY were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 1 grafting-tool, value 4s., the goods of James Layton.
PETER DANIELS. I and the prisoners are navigators - they worked in the same field as me, but not at the same spot. Layton left this tool with me about seven o'clock on Saturday evening, and I put it under the stairs at the Falcon public-house, Stoke Newington, where I sleep - I missed it about half-past six on Sunday evening - the prisoners were drinking in the tap-room; I went to them and said, "It is very strange you should go into the place and take the tool; it must be somebody who is in the house;" Hadiston followed me outside the door and said, "If you say we stole the tool I will smash you," (holding up his clenched fist) - I said I did not say it was them, but it must be somebody in the house.
DANIEL DAWSON. I keep a retail beer-shop in Stoke Newington. The two prisoner came into the passage leading to my tap-room, in company - I asked what they wanted, they said the boy I employed to take out the beer- I said he was gone out, and asked what they wanted of him - one of them (I don't know which) said, to leave this tool till Monday morning - I said I would take care of it, as they used my house.
JOSEPH MARSHALL. I am a Police-constable. I apprehended the prisoners on the 28th of March; they said nothing to me.
EDWARD FREEMAN. I got the tool from the beer-shop- I have had it ever since.
JAMES LAYTON. This is my tool; I left it in Daniel's care.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How do you know it? A. It is marked with a piece of wire at each end - I never asked for 30s. not to prosecute - Thomas McBride came to me and offered me 30s., but I never asked him for it - I do not know whether any body else was present. but nobody can say I asked him for the money.
Hadiston's Defence. I am innocent.
THOMAS McBRIDE. I offered to settle this with Layton - he asked me 5l. at first - he told me if I would not give that before he went before the Magistrate, he would prosecute the men - there had been a subscription raised among their friends.
COURT. Q. Did Daniels or Layton want to make it up? - A.Daniels wanted 7s. for two days' work, and Layton wanted 30s. - they had been before the Magistrate once - the prisoners knew themselves innocent, and did not wish the money to be given, but our mates subscribed 30s. - we could not afford to raise 5l. - he afterwards said he would take 30s., which we put into the hands of Mr. Hudson - he said he would keep away from the Magistrate for 3s. 6d. a day.
JURY to PETER DANIELS. Q. Did you find the tool at a different house to where you lost it from? A. Yes, I found it at a beer-shop one hundred and fifty yards off.
HADISTON - GUILTY. Aged 29.
Confined Three Weeks.
GARRATY - GUILTY. Aged 30.
Confined Seven Days.
[trialAccount t18310407-271]
966. JOSEPH JORDAN was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of February, 1 coat, value 10s., and 1 pair of boots, value 10s., the goods of Robert Proctor.
SARAH FOX. I am servant to Mr. Proctor, of Perceval-street. On the 22nd of February we were having wine in, between six and seven o'clock in the evening - my master desired me to stand at the street-door, which was open, while it was brought in, and as I went up, I saw a man going out of the passage with a coat and pair of shoes, which were taken out of the hall - I gave an alarm; my master went out, and took him.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q.How far off was he taken? A. About twenty yards - I did not lose sight of him; the boots were not found, but I saw him with them as well as the coat.
ROBERT PROCTOR. I was coming out of my dining-room, and the servant said, "There is a man run away with your boots;" she pointed out the prisoner, who was running about twenty yards off - I ran, took him into a shop, and said, "Where are my boots?" he said, "I have no boots of your's;" he was very uneasy, and shuffling a coat off his back; I found he had my coat on over his own; it was rather dusky - he kept shaking his arms as he ran, and I suppose he was then putting the coat on - the boots have not been found.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Seven Years.

968. MARTIN McNORTY was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of John Fenwick, from his person.
JOHN FENWICK. I am a silk-mercer, and live in the Regent-circus. On the 6th of April, about eleven o'clock in the morning, I was in Holborn; I felt a pull at my pocket, and missed my handkerchief; I turned round, and saw the prisoner close to my elbows - I followed him a fewpaces; the Policeman came up, and took him by my desire - I said he had my handkerchief; he denied it - I saw it found in his trousers pocket.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18310407-274]
969. WILLIAM WALKER was indicted for embezzlement.
THOMAS DINSDALE. I am an agent and clerk at St. Katharine's-wharf - the prisoner was occasionally my agent, or servant; I paid him as he worked. Some rags were sent to me for sale; he told me he had sold them to Rackley for 19l. 6s. 1d., according to their weight - I gave him a note to Rackley for the money; I had sent the goods by my team on the 31st of March, about twelve o'clock in the day, and was to have the money back the same day; I authorized the prisoner to receive it for me- I had paid him 6s. that day for previous work which he had done; he never brought me the money - I told him to bring it to me or my employer; I did not see him again till the morning following, when I went with an officer, and took him out of bed.
Prisoner. Mr. Dinsdale received 1l. on account. Witness. I received 1l. from Rackley the day previous to his getting the money; I got it to pay the prisoner the 6s.
JOHN RACKLEY. I bought the rags of the prisoner as agent for Dinsdale - they came to 19l. 6s. 1d.; I had paid 1l. on account, and paid the prisoner 18l. 6s. 1d. on the 31st of March - here is a receipt which he signed for it in my presence (read); I paid him the whole amount, but finding 1l. had been paid, he returned that to me.
MR. DINSDALE. He was to take the money to my employer if I was not in the way - I have not received any of it.
Prisoner's Defence I received it on Good Friday, and on Saturday was going down to pay Mr. Dinsdale -I got intoxicated, and was robbed on Saturday evening; on Sunday morning I took the party who robbed me to the station-house in Wellclose-square; I was to appear against them at Lambeth-street on Monday, but Mr. Dinsdale came with an officer and took me out of bed.
MR. DINSDALE. After he was apprehended, he took a German there who could not speak English, and said he had robbed him; the Magistrate was three hours investigating the case, and then said there was not the least charge against the man, and I must prosecute the prisoner - the prisoner said the man and him bad had a glass of rum together, and that ten or twelve people were in the house when he was robbed, but it was proved he was not robbed at all.
GUILTY. Aged 45. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18310407-275]
970. SARAH BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1 sheet, value 7s., and 1 spoon, value 2s., the goods of John Gibson; and 1 spoon, value 2s., the goods of Ann Garlick.
ANN DUTT. I live in Hornsey-row, Islington. Mr. John Gibson and Ann Garlick lodge with me - the prisoner had been rather better than twelve months in Mr. Gibson's service; I had reason to suspect her - the officer searched her box on the 3rd on the April, and found several duplicates.
JOHN NEAL. I am a Policeman. I found six duplicates in the prisoner's box, one for a shirt and a spoon, and I found duplicates of five more spoons on her person.
GEORGE STOWELL. I am a pawnbroker, and live at Islington. I have a shirt and two spoons, pawned by the prisoner at different times.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury.
Confined Three Months.
